59.00912pt\raise-31.17557pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 63.28159pt\raise-51.35114pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}}}

The following is an immediate consequence of the definitions.

Proposition 4.3
.

Suppose that
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is a functor that reflects amalgamation of squares and that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
is an independence relation on
\mathcal{D}
satisfying uniqueness, then
F^{-1}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}})
satisfies uniqueness.

Example 4.4
.

In addition to the forgetful functor
F:\operatorname{\bf Bil}_{K}\to\operatorname{\bf Vec}_{K}
from Example
3.1
(1), we also consider the following functor. Let
\operatorname{\bf BinFunc}_{K}
be the category whose objects are sets
X
with a binary function
X\times X\to K
into
K
, and the morphisms are injections of these sets that respect the binary function, then we take
G:\operatorname{\bf Bil}_{K}\to\operatorname{\bf BinFunc}_{K}
to be the functor that forgets about the vector space structure. Neither
F
nor
G
reflects amalgamation of squares, but
\langle F,G\rangle:\operatorname{\bf Bil}_{K}\to\operatorname{\bf Vec}_{K}\times\operatorname{\bf BinFunc}_{K}
does.

The situation in the above example where we really have to consider a product of categories happens often in practice, so it will be useful to give this a name.

Definition 4.5
.

We say that a family of functors
\{F_{j}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}_{j}\}_{j\in J}

jointly reflect amalgamation of squares
if
\langle F_{j}\rangle_{j\in J}:\mathcal{C}\to\prod_{j\in J}\mathcal{D}_{j}
reflects amalgamation of squares.

Note that the above condition is further equivalent to saying that two squares in
\mathcal{C}
can be amalgamated if and only if their images under each
F_{j}
can be amalgamated.

Remark 4.6
.

We compare the amalgamation of squares to the model-theoretic notion of (Galois) types (see e.g.,
[
13
, Section 3]
). This remark is not used anywhere else in this paper, so the reader may choose to skip it.

We recall that two tuples of morphisms
(a_{i}:A_{i}\to D)_{i\in I}
and
(a^{\prime}_{i}:A_{i}\to D^{\prime})_{i\in I}
have the same Galois type if they can be amalgamated, in the sense that there are
D\xrightarrow{f}E\xleftarrow{g}D^{\prime}
such that
fa_{i}=ga^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i\in I
. If a category has the amalgamation property this is an equivalence relation. Being able to amalgamate two squares is then a special case of having the same Galois type.

In the model-theoretic context, having the same Galois type often admits a syntactic description. For example, if the category in question is the category
\operatorname{\bf Mod}(T)
of models of a first-order theory
T
with elementary embeddings then two tuples of morphisms have the same Galois type if and only if we can enumerate their images such that these enumerations satisfy the same first-order formulas (such a set of formulas satisfied by a tuple is called a
type
). This works similarly for categories of models in some other logics, such as positive logic
[
4
]
or continuous logic
[
5
]
. Note that these categories always have the amalgamation property.

We can thus define a functor
F
between categories with the amalgamation property to be
Galois type reflecting
if two tuples of morphisms have the same Galois type if and only if their images under
F
have the same Galois type. This is more general than reflecting amalgamation of squares, but is in practice sometimes easier to check. Example
4.4
can be cast in this language: take two tuples of monomorphisms of bilinear spaces
(a_{i}:A_{i}\to D)_{i\in I}
and
(a^{\prime}_{i}:A_{i}\to D^{\prime})_{i\in I}
, and view each
a_{i}
as a tuple enumerating its image in
D
(similarly for the
a^{\prime}_{i}
). Then these can be amalgamated in
\operatorname{\bf Bil}_{K}
if and only if:

(1)

\operatorname{span}(a_{i}:i\in I)\cong\operatorname{span}(a^{\prime}_{i}:i\in I)
, that is,
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
and
(a^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}
satisfy the same
K
-linear equations;

(2)

[x,y]=[x^{\prime},y^{\prime}]
for all
x,y
singletons in the concatenated tuple
(a_{i})_{i\in I}
and
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}
of matching positions in
(a^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}
, where
[-,-]
is the
K
-bilinear form on
D
(and on
D^{\prime}
, by overloading notation).

Model-theoretically we can say that the type of a tuple is determined by the type of that tuple in the underlying vector space together with the type of that tuple with respect to the bilinear form (seen as just a binary function).

Corollary 4.7
.

Suppose that
\{F_{j}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}_{j}\}_{j\in J}
is a family of functors that jointly reflect amalgamation of squares and suppose that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{j}
is an independence relation on each
\mathcal{D}_{j}
satisfying uniqueness. Let
\bigcap_{j\in J}F_{j}^{-1}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{j})
be the independence relation on
\mathcal{C}
where a square is independent if it is
F_{j}^{-1}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{j})
-independent for all
j\in J
. Then
\bigcap_{j\in J}F_{j}^{-1}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{j})
satisfies uniqueness.

Proof.

Define an independence relation
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
on
\prod_{j\in J}\mathcal{D}_{j}
by declaring a square independent if and only if its
j
th component is
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{j}
-independent in
\mathcal{D}_{j}
for all
j\in J
. It is straightforward to check that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
satisfies uniqueness. Then
\bigcap_{j\in J}F_{j}^{-1}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{j})=\langle F_{j}\rangle_{j\in J}^{-1}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}})
and
\langle F_{j}\rangle_{j\in J}
reflects amalgamation of squares, so the result follows from Proposition

4.3
.
∎

Example 4.8
.

We continue Example
4.4
. We define
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
on
\operatorname{\bf BinFunc}_{K}
as follows. For a set
X
with a binary function
f:X\times X\to K
, and subsets
A,B,C\subseteq X
with
C\subseteq A\cap B
we set
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{X}_{C}B
if and only if
A\cap B=C
and for any
a\in A\setminus C
and
b\in B\setminus C
we have that
f(a,b)=f(b,a)=0
. It is well-known that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\operatorname{lin}}
on
\operatorname{\bf Vec}_{K}
satisfies uniqueness and it is easy to check that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
on
\operatorname{\bf BinFunc}_{K}
satisfies uniqueness. Applying Proposition

4.7
to the forgetful functors
F:\operatorname{\bf Bil}_{K}\to\operatorname{\bf Vec}_{K}
and
G:\operatorname{\bf Bil}_{K}\to\operatorname{\bf BinFunc}_{K}
these independence relations lift to an independence relation
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{*}
on
\operatorname{\bf Bil}_{K}
, which satisfies uniqueness. This can also be straightforwardly verified directly from the explicit description of
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{*}
on
\operatorname{\bf Bil}_{K}
: for
A,B,C\subseteq V
subspaces of a bilinear space
V
with
C\subseteq A\cap B
we have that
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{*,V}_{C}B
if and only if
A\cap B=C
and for any
a\in A\setminus C
and
b\in B\setminus C
we have that
[a,b]=[b,a]=0
, where
[-,-]
is the bilinear form on
V
. Note that this is different from the canonical independence relation on
\operatorname{\bf Bil}_{K}
, which is
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\operatorname{lin}}
.

We finish this section by considering a condition that implies reflection of amalgamation of squares, and much more. This condition is a weakening of being a left adjoint, which we recall here (see also
[
1
, Definition 4.24(1)]
, dualised).

Definition 4.9
.

A functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is called a
left multiadjoint
if for every object
D
in
\mathcal{D}
there is a family of morphisms
\{e_{i}:F(C_{i})\to D\}_{i\in I}
such that for every
e:F(C)\to D
there is a unique
i\in I
and unique
f:C\to C_{i}
such that
e_{i}F(f)=e
.

As mentioned before: being a left adjoint is a special case of being a left multiadjoint. If
F
is left adjoint to
R:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
then for any
D
in
\mathcal{D}
one can take the singleton
\{\varepsilon_{D}:FR(D)\to D\}
to be the family of morphisms, where
\varepsilon:FR\to Id
is the counit of the adjunction. Then given any
e:F(C)\to D
the unique
f
is given by
F(\tilde{e}):F(C)\to FR(D)
, where
\tilde{e}
is the transpose of
e
under the adjunction.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a left multiadjoint, let
(X_{i})_{i\in I}
be a nonempty connected diagram in
\mathcal{C}
and let
(d_{i}:F(X_{i})\to D)_{i\in I}
be a cocone in
\mathcal{D}
. Then there is a cocone
(f_{i}:X_{i}\to C)_{i\in I}
in
\mathcal{C}
and
u:F(C)\to D
such that
d_{i}=uF(f_{i})
for all
i\in I
.

Furthermore, for any other cocone
(f^{\prime}_{i}:X_{i}\to C^{\prime})_{i\in I}
such that there is
u^{\prime}:F(C^{\prime})\to D
with
d_{i}=u^{\prime}F(f^{\prime}_{i})
for all
i\in I
there is a unique
g:C^{\prime}\to C
such that
f_{i}=gf^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i\in I
.

Proof.

Let
\{e_{j}:F(C_{j})\to D\}_{j\in J}
be the family of morphisms coming from the fact that
F
is a left multiadjoint. For any
i\in I
we let
j_{i}\in J
be the unique index such that there is a unique
f_{i}:X_{i}\to C_{i_{j}}
with
d_{i}=e_{j_{i}}F(f_{i})
. We claim that
j_{i}=j_{i^{\prime}}
for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
. As the diagram is connected, any
i,i^{\prime}\in I
can be connected by a zig-zag of morphisms
i=i_{1}\to i_{2}\leftarrow i_{3}\to\ldots\leftarrow i_{n}=i^{\prime}
. Then for every
1\leq k<n
we have that one of
d_{i_{k}}
and
d_{i_{k+1}}
factors through the other. So by uniqueness
j_{i_{k}}=j_{i_{k+1}}
, and the claim follows.

We can thus take any
i^{*}\in I
and we set
C=C_{j_{i^{*}}}
and
u=e_{j_{i^{*}}}
, where nonemptiness of the diagram implies that there is at least one
{i^{*}}\in I
. Finally, uniqueness of the
f_{i}
’s then implies that
(f_{i}:X_{i}\to C)_{i\in I}
is in fact a cocone.

For the furthermore part, we let
j^{\prime}\in J
be such that
u^{\prime}:F(C^{\prime})\to D
factors as
u^{\prime}=e_{j^{\prime}}F(g)
for some
g:C^{\prime}\to C_{j^{\prime}}
. Since
d_{i^{*}}
factors through
u^{\prime}
by assumption, we have by uniqueness that
j^{\prime}=j_{i^{*}}
. The fact that
f_{i}=gf^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i\in I
follows from the uniqueness of the
f_{i}
’s.
∎

Multicolimits
generalise colimits in the same way as left
multiadjoints generalise left adjoints, i.e., the initial cocone is replaced by a multiinitial set of cocones (see, e.g.,
[
1
, Definition 4.24]
). The following corollary generalises
[
1
, Theorem 4.26(i)]
.

Corollary 4.11
.

Left multiadjoints preserve connected multicolimits.

Often, an independence relation arises by starting with a locally presentable category and then passing to an accessible subcategory by restricting the morphisms. For example,
[
20
, Theorem 5.1]
states that if
\mathcal{C}
is a coregular locally presentable category with effective unions then
\mathcal{C}_{\operatorname{Reg}}
has a stable independence relation. The subcategory is generally no longer locally presentable, and thus less likely to be the domain or codomain of a left (multi)adjoint, because we lose completeness and cocompleteness of the categories and so theorems such as the adjoint functor theorem no longer apply.

Proposition 4.12
.

Let
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a left multiadjoint and let
\mathcal{M}
be a left-cancellable composable class of morphisms in
\mathcal{D}
. Then the restriction
F:\mathcal{C}_{F^{-1}(\mathcal{M})}\to\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}
reflects amalgamation of squares.

Proof.

We start with two squares in
\mathcal{C}_{F^{-1}(\mathcal{M})}
that share their base span, and we assume that their images under
F
can be amalgamated in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}
. Then, working in
\mathcal{D}
we find the commuting diagram below.

\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{F(E_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{F(D^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F(E_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F(C)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Here the solid arrows are the ones we started with (including amalgamation of the squares in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}
). Then, applying Lemma
4.10
to the diagram
A\leftarrow C\to B
, we find
D^{\prime}
and the dashed arrows. Applying the “furthermore” part of Lemma
4.10
yields the dotted arrows.

Lemma
4.10
also tells us that all the morphisms between objects in the image of
F
come from morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
, and such that the diagram in
\mathcal{C}
commutes. Furthermore,
\mathcal{M}
is left-cancellable, so since the solid arrows are all in
\mathcal{M}
we have that the dotted arrows are in
\mathcal{M}
. We thus see that
D^{\prime}
amalgamates the squares in
\mathcal{C}_{F^{-1}(\mathcal{M})}
, as required.
∎

Theorem 4.13
.

Let
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a left multiadjoint and let
\mathcal{M}
be a left-cancellable composable class of morphisms in
\mathcal{D}
. If
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
is an independence relation on
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}
that satisfies uniqueness then the independence relation
F^{-1}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}})
on
\mathcal{C}_{F^{-1}(\mathcal{M})}
satisfies uniqueness.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.12

F
reflects amalgamation of squares, so the result follows from Proposition
4.3
.
∎

Examples 4.14
.

We consider examples of functors
U:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
that are left adjoint (and thus in particular left multiadjoint) together with a class of morphisms
\mathcal{M}
on
\mathcal{D}
such that
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}
carries an independence relation that satisfies uniqueness. So Theorem
4.13
applies.

(1)

We consider the forgetful functor
U:\operatorname{\bf Gra}\to\operatorname{\bf Set}
, where
\operatorname{\bf Gra}
is the category of graphs and graph homomorphisms. This functor has a right adjoint given by taking complete graphs. We let
\mathcal{M}
be the class of monomorphisms in
\operatorname{\bf Set}
, so
U^{-1}(\mathcal{M})
is the class of monomorphisms in
\operatorname{\bf Gra}
.

Pullback squares form an independence relation on
\operatorname{\bf Set}_{\operatorname{Mono}}
that satisfies uniqueness. As
U
preserves and reflects pullback squares, we have that pullback squares also satisfy uniqueness as an independence relation on
\operatorname{\bf Gra}_{\operatorname{Mono}}
. Note that this is not the case of
\operatorname{\bf Gra}_{\operatorname{Emb}}
, where there morphisms are graph embeddings. There uniqueness is known to fail for pullback squares.

(2)

Let
\mathcal{D}
be
\operatorname{\bf Set}
(resp.
\operatorname{\bf Ab}
, the category of abelian groups) and let
\mathcal{C}
be the category
\sigma\text{-}\operatorname{\bf Set}
of sets with an endomorphism (resp.
\sigma\text{-}\operatorname{\bf Ab}
, the category of abelian groups with an endomorphism). Then the forgetful functor
U:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
preserves limits and colimits, and both
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
are locally presentable. Thus
U
is a left adjoint, by
[
1
, Theorem 1.58]
and the Special Adjoint Functor Theorem. Pullback squares form an independence relation on
\mathcal{D}_{\operatorname{Mono}}
that satisfies uniqueness. As
U
preserves and reflects pullback squares, we have that pullback squares also satisfy uniqueness in
\mathcal{C}_{\operatorname{Mono}}
.

(3)

Let
\operatorname{\bf Coalg}_{R}
be the category of coalgebras over a ring
R
. The forgetful functor
U:\operatorname{\bf Coalg}_{R}\to\operatorname{\bf Mod}_{R}
to modules over
R
has a right adjoint. Let
\mathcal{M}
consist of morphisms sent by
U
to monomorphisms (
\mathcal{M}
is properly contained in
\operatorname{Mono}
). Since
\operatorname{\bf Mod}_{R}
has effective unions,
(\operatorname{\bf Mod}_{R})_{\operatorname{Mono}}
has a stable independence relation given by pullback squares
[
21
, Theorem 3.1]
. In particular the pullback squares satisfy uniqueness, and so the squares in
(\operatorname{\bf Coalg}_{R})_{\mathcal{M}}
that are sent to pullback squares by
U
satisfy uniqueness (in fact, they form a stable independence relation, see Theorem
9.4
).

Examples 4.15
.

We give examples of left multiadjoints that are not left adjoints and to which Theorem
4.13
applies.

(1)

Fix some
0<n<\omega
and a field
K
. Let
\mathcal{C}
be the category of vector spaces over
K
with a predicate for a linear subspace of dimension
n
, where the morphisms are embeddings. That is, its objects are pairs
(U,V)
with
U
a linear subspace of the vector space
V
such that
\dim(V/U)=n
. A morphism
f:(U,V)\to(U^{\prime},V^{\prime})
is a linear injection
f:V\to V^{\prime}
such that
f(v)\in U^{\prime}
if and only if
v\in U
. Write
\mathcal{D}
for the category
\operatorname{\bf Vec}_{K}\times\operatorname{\bf Vec}_{K}
with the morphisms restricted to monomorphisms. We consider the functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
, given by
F(U,V)=(U,V/U)
with the obvious action on morphisms. This is a left multiadjoint: given
(U,W)
in
\mathcal{D}
we let
\{W_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be the family of
n
-dimensional linear subspaces of
W
. For each
i\in I
we consider the object
(U,U\times W_{i})
of
\mathcal{C}
and let
e_{i}:F(U,U\times W_{i})\to(U,W)
be the inclusion
F(U,U\times W_{i})\cong(U,W_{i})\hookrightarrow(U,W)
. Then the family
\{e_{i}:F(U,U\times W_{i})\to(U,W)\}_{i\in I}
witnesses that
F
is a left multiadjoint. The independence relation on
\mathcal{D}
given by
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{\operatorname{lin}}
on each component then lifts along
F
to an independence relation on
\mathcal{C}
that satisfies uniqueness.

To see that
F
is not a left adjoint we note that it is full and faithful. So if it were a left adjoint, we could view
\mathcal{C}
as a coreflective subcategory of
\mathcal{D}
. So
\mathcal{C}
would be closed under coproducts in
\mathcal{D}
, but for example the coproduct of
(0,K^{n})
and
(0,K^{n})
does not exist in
\mathcal{C}
.

(2)

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a small category. We say that a presheaf
K:\mathcal{A}^{\operatorname{op}}\to\operatorname{\bf Set}
is
connected
if for any
a\in K(A)
and
a^{\prime}\in K(A^{\prime})
there are
a=a_{0},a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}=a^{\prime}
in the image of
K
such that for all
0\leq i<n
there is a morphism
f
in
\mathcal{A}
with
K(f)(a_{i})=a_{i+1}
or
K(f)(a_{i+1})=a_{i}
. Write
\mathcal{C}
for the full subcategory of
\operatorname{\bf Set}^{\mathcal{A}^{\operatorname{op}}}
of connected presheaves. The the inclusion functor
F:\mathcal{C}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{\bf Set}^{\mathcal{A}^{\operatorname{op}}}
is a left multiadjoint. Indeed, given a presheaf
K
, the inclusions of connected components of
K
(i.e., maximal connected subpresheaves) witness that
F
is a left multiadjoint. The pullback squares form an independence relation on
\operatorname{\bf Set}^{\mathcal{A}^{\operatorname{op}}}_{\operatorname{Mono}}
that satisfies uniqueness
[
20
, Example 5.3]
. So the pullback squares also form an independence relation on
\mathcal{C}_{\operatorname{Mono}}
that satisfies uniqueness. Finally, we note that
F
cannot be a left adjoint, because then
\mathcal{C}
would be a coreflective subcategory of
\operatorname{\bf Set}^{\mathcal{A}^{\operatorname{op}}}
and thus has to be closed under colimits in
\operatorname{\bf Set}^{\mathcal{A}^{\operatorname{op}}}
. However,
\mathcal{C}
is clearly not closed under coproducts in
\operatorname{\bf Set}^{\mathcal{A}^{\operatorname{op}}}
.

(3)

We finish with a general construction that yields a left multiadjoint that is rarely a left adjoint. Let
\{F_{j}:\mathcal{C}_{j}\to\mathcal{D}\}_{j\in J}
be a family of left (multi)adjoints. Then the induced functor
F:\coprod_{j\in J}\mathcal{C}_{j}\to\mathcal{D}
is a left multiadjoint. To see this, we fix an object
D
in
\mathcal{D}
. For each
j\in J
there is a family
\{e^{j}_{i}:F(C^{j}_{i})\to D\}_{i\in I_{j}}
witnessing that
F_{j}
is a left multiadjoint. We claim that
\{e^{j}_{i}\}_{j\in J,i\in I_{j}}
witnesses that
F
is a left multiadjoint. Let
e:F(C)\to D
be some morphism. Then
C
is an object in
\mathcal{C}_{j}
for some
j\in J
. Hence
e
factors as
e^{j}_{i}F(f)
for some
i\in I_{j}
and
f:C\to C^{j}_{i}
. Clearly, there are no solutions for this factorisation problem in
I_{j^{\prime}}
or
\mathcal{C}_{j^{\prime}}
, for
j^{\prime}\neq j
. So
i
and
f
remain the unique solution.

An
F
as above is rarely a left adjoint. For example, if
\mathcal{D}
has the
joint continuation property
: any two objects
A
and
B
admit a cospan into a third object
A\to C\leftarrow B
. Let
j,j^{\prime}\in J
be distinct (of course, if
|J|=1
we are in a trivial case) and pick objects
C_{j}
and
C_{j^{\prime}}
in
\mathcal{C}_{j}
and
\mathcal{C}_{j^{\prime}}
respectively (again, we exclude the trivial case where one of the categories is empty). Let
D
be such that there are morphisms
F(C_{j})\to D\leftarrow F(C_{j^{\prime}})
. If
F
were a left adjoint, with right adjoint
R:\mathcal{D}\to\coprod_{j\in J}\mathcal{C}_{j}
, then we would get a diagram
C_{j}\to R(D)\leftarrow C_{j^{\prime}}
in
\coprod_{j\in J}\mathcal{C}_{j}
, which is impossible because
C_{j}
and
C_{j^{\prime}}
live in different connected components.

To give a concrete example of this construction we consider
\mathcal{C}=\coprod_{n<\omega}\operatorname{\bf Set}^{\mathbb{Z}^{n}}
, the category of sets with a finite number of automorphisms. By the same argument as in Example
4.14
(2) the forgetful functor
\operatorname{\bf Set}^{\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\to\operatorname{\bf Set}
is left adjoint for each
n<\omega
. By the above the forgetful functor
U:\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{\bf Set}
is a left multiadjoint that is not left adjoint. The independence relation on
\operatorname{\bf Set}_{\operatorname{Mono}}
given by pullback squares then lifts to an independence relation (again given by pullback squares) on
\mathcal{C}
that satisfies uniqueness.

5.
Lifting existence

Left multiadjoints will generally also lift the existence property, in a similar way to how they lift the uniqueness property. As in that setting, we will want to consider a category
\mathcal{C}
and some class of morphisms
\mathcal{M}
, where
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{M}}
actually carries the independence relation
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
(see also the discussion before Proposition
4.12
). We can also view
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
as an independence relation on
\mathcal{C}
by just taking the same collection of squares, but it will lose certain properties, such as invariance. In practice we often retain a weaker version of invariance.

Definition 5.1
.

An independence relation
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
on some category
\mathcal{C}
is said to satisfy
semi-invariance
if given any commuting diagram like below, the outer square being
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
-independent implies that the inner square is
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
-independent.

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

The philosophy behind the above definition is that if something was already dependent in
D
, such as some equation holding, then its image in
E
is also dependent, as morphisms generally preserve truth of things like equations. The contrapositive of this statement is exactly what we called semi-invariance. Invariance is then the statement that independence is also preserved, something that goes wrong more quickly, as the following example illustrates.

Example 5.2
.

Let
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
on
\operatorname{\bf Set}_{\operatorname{Mono}}
be given by pullback squares. This is easily seen to have invariance. However,
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
as an independence relation on
\operatorname{\bf Set}
consists of all those pullback squares whose morphisms are monomorphisms. As such,
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
will no longer satisfies invariance as is illustrated in the diagram below: the inner square is a pullback, but the outer square is not, and so independence is not preserved upwards along the dashed arrow.

\textstyle{\{a\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\{a,b\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\{*\}}
\textstyle{\;\emptyset\;\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\{b\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

However,
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
on
\operatorname{\bf Set}
does satisfy semi-invariance, as is straightforwardly verified.

Theorem 5.3
.

Let
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a left multiadjoint and let
\mathcal{M}
be a composable class of morphisms in
\mathcal{D}
. Suppose that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
is an independence relation on
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}
that satisfies existence and that it satisfies semi-invariance as an independence relation on
\mathcal{D}
. Then
F^{-1}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}})
as an independence relation on
\mathcal{C}_{F^{-1}(\mathcal{M})}
satisfies existence.

Proof.

Let
A\leftarrow C\to B
be any span in
\mathcal{C}_{F^{-1}(\mathcal{M})}
. We then find the commuting diagram below.

\textstyle{F(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{F(D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{E}
\textstyle{F(C)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

By existence for
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
we find
E
and the dashed arrows, such that the outer square is
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
-independent. We then apply Lemma
4.10
to the span
A\leftarrow C\to B
to find
D
and the dotted arrows, making everything commute.

Furthermore, Lemma
4.10
also implies that the morphisms between objects in the image of
F
come from morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
, and such that the diagram in
\mathcal{C}
commutes. By semi-invariance the inner square is
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
-independent. In particular, that means that it lies in the image of the inclusion
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{M}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{D}
. Therefore, the square with
A,B,C,D
in
\mathcal{C}
also lies in
\mathcal{C}_{F^{-1}(\mathcal{M})}
and is
F^{-1}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}})
-independent, as required.
∎

We give another sufficient criterion to lift existence, which is motivated by practical examples.

Definition 5.4
.

Let
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a faithful functor. Let
f:F(A)\to B
be a morphism in
\mathcal{D}
. A
1
-completion
is a morphism
g:A\to C
in
\mathcal{C}
such that
F(g):F(A)\to F(C)
factors through
f
(i.e.,
F(g)=hf
for some
h:B\to F(C)
). We say that
F

admits
1
-completions
if for any
F(A)\to B
in
\mathcal{D}
there is a
1
-completion.

\textstyle{F(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{F(g)}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{F(C)}

For the intuition behind
1
-completions we consider the forgetful functor
F:\operatorname{\bf Bil}_{K}\to\operatorname{\bf Vec}_{K}
from Example
3.1
(1). Suppose that we have a vector space
V
and a subspace
U\subseteq V
with a bilinear form
[-,-]
defined on
U
. Then we can extend
[-,-]
to all of
V
, making
V
into a bilinear space
V^{\prime}
. In terms of the functor
F
this corresponds to having a morphism
F(U)\to V
and then we find a morphism
V\to F(V^{\prime})
(in fact, the identity in this case) such that
F(U\to V^{\prime})
is the composite
F(U)\to V\to F(V^{\prime})
.

More generally, admitting
1
-completions says the following. Suppose that we are given an object
B
from
\mathcal{D}
on which the extra structure that objects in
\mathcal{C}
carry is partially defined, namely as some
F(A)\to B
. Then
B
can be completed to an object
F(C)
from
\mathcal{C}
in a way that is compatible with the already defined structure.

Now consider the following higher dimensional situation for
F:\operatorname{\bf Bil}_{K}\to\operatorname{\bf Vec}_{K}
. Suppose we have a vector space
V
with a subspaces
U,U_{1},U_{2}\subseteq V
such that
U\subseteq U_{1}\cap U_{2}
and each carries a bilinear form, with the bilinear forms on
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
extending the one on
U
. To extend all these bilinear forms to one bilinear form on
V
we generally need
U_{1}
to be linearly independent from
U_{2}
over
U
, because that gives us complete freedom in how we define the bilinear form on pairs of vectors
(u_{1},u_{2})
and
(u_{2},u_{1})
with
u_{1}\in U_{1}\setminus U
and
u_{2}\in U_{2}\setminus U
. Diagrammatically, this means that given an independent square in
\operatorname{\bf Vec}_{K}
like below on the left there is some commuting square in
\operatorname{\bf Bil}_{K}
like below on the right whose image under
F
is the square on the left.

\vbox{\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 42.40414pt\raise-5.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}}}

Definition 5.5
.

Let
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a faithful functor. Suppose we are given a commuting square in
\mathcal{D}
like the solid arrows in the diagram below, where the morphisms between objects in the image of
F
are also in the image of
F
. A
2
-completion
for that square consists of an object
E
and the dashed arrows making the diagram below commute.

\textstyle{F(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f)}
\textstyle{D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{F(E)}
\textstyle{F(C)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}}
\textstyle{F(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(g)}

Given an independence relation
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
on
\mathcal{D}
we say that
F

admits
2
-completions with respect to
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
if for any
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
-independent square in
\mathcal{D}
there is a
2
-completion. There will often only be one relevant independence relation
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
, in which case we will just say that
F
admits
2
-completions.

We note that faithfulness of
F
implies that commutativity of the diagrams in the above definition means that the corresponding square in
\mathcal{C}
(i.e., the one involving
C
,
A
,
B
and
E
) also commutes.

Observe that our notion of admitting
2
-completions is weaker than the notion of cofinality given in
[
22
, Definition 2.4]
, even if we would fix the shape of the diagram there. This is because we require the diagram to also be independent, the necessity of which we discussed before Definition
5.5
. That being said, the different notions have different intended applications, as is indicated by the different names.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a faithful functor and suppose that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
is an independence relation on
\mathcal{D}
satisfying basic existence. If
F
admits
2
-completions it admits
1
-completions.

Proof.

This is done by inserting identity morphisms in the right places, which is why we need basic existence to have that these squares be independent.
∎

Theorem 5.7
.

Let
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
be a faithful functor and suppose that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
is an independence relation on
\mathcal{D}
that satisfies invariance.

(1)

If
F
admits
2
-completions and
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
satisfies existence then
F^{-1}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}})
satisfies existence.

(2)

If
F
admits
1
-completions,
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
satisfies uniqueness and
F^{-1}(\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}})
satisfies existence then
F
admits
2
-completions.

Proof.

We first prove (1). Let
A\leftarrow C\to B
be a span in
\mathcal{C}
. By existence and admitting
2
-completions we can recreate the diagram from Definition
5.5
. By invariance the commuting square below on the left is independent. As
F
is faithful, the square on the right also commutes and is thus an independent square, as required.

\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}}

6.
Strong 3-amalgamation

Definition 6.1
.

We say that an independence relation
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
on a category
\mathcal{C}
satisfies
strong
3
-amalgamation
if given an
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
-independent horn like the solid arrows below, there are
N
and the dashed arrows such that everything commutes and every square in the resulting cube is
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
-independent (we call this an
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
-independent cube
).

\textstyle{N_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

The name
strong
3-amalgamation is because in the definition of
3
-amalgamation it is only required that
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{N}_{M}N_{3}
. If
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
satisfies transitivity (which the independence relations we consider generally will) then the independence of this diagonal square automatically follows. Conversely, it turns out that under reasonable assumptions
3
-amalgamation implies strong
3
-amalgamation, which we will prove in the remainder of this section.

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category whose morphisms are monomorphisms that has binary joins of subobjects and suppose that it is equipped with an independence relation
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
that satisfies invariance, monotonicity, transitivity, symmetry, existence, base monotonicity and
3
-amalgamation. Then
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
satisfies strong
3
-amalgamation.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}
be as in Theorem
6.2
. Then given a commuting cube like below on the left with
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{N}_{M}N_{3}
there are
N\to N^{\prime}
and
f:N_{2}\to N^{\prime}
such that the square below on the right is independent

\textstyle{N_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N^{\prime}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{N_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{N^{\prime}}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}}
\textstyle{N_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

and such that
A\to N_{2}\to N\to N^{\prime}
is the same as
A\to N_{2}\xrightarrow{f}N^{\prime}
, and similarly with
C
in place of

.

Note that the above lemma does not claim that the diagram with the dashed arrows commutes. Also, the proof of the above lemma does not use
3
-amalgamation or joins of subobjects.

Proof.

We build the up the commuting diagram below in a few steps.

\textstyle{N^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{N^{\prime}}
\textstyle{N_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}}
\textstyle{N^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}}
\textstyle{N_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

(1)

We start with the solid arrows.

(2)

Applying base monotonicity to
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{N}_{M}N_{3}
we find the dashed arrows, such that
P\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}^{N^{*}}_{C}N_{3}
.

(3)

By commutativity of the original cube and what we have constructed so far, we have that
A\to N_{2}\to N\to N^{*}
is the same as
A\to P\to N^{*}
, and similarly with
C
in place of
A
. This yields the dotted arrows:
\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{N^{*}}
is the same as
P\dashrightarrow N^{*}
and
\textstyle{N_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{\cdot}
\textstyle{N^{*}}
is the composition

.

(4)

Applying existence to
\textstyle{N^{*}}
\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{N^{*}}
we find the squigly arrows making the top square independent.

We take the unnamed morphism
N\to N^{\prime}
to be the composition
N\to N^{*}\xrightarrow{h}N^{\prime}
and we take
f:N_{2}\to N^{\prime}
to be the composition
N_{2}\to N^{*}\xrightarrow{g}N^{\prime}
. By transitivity (and symmetry) the right rectangle in the above diagram is independent, so by monotonicity we have that the required square with
f
is indeed independent. Straightforwardly writing out definitions then verifies the claim about the morphisms with domains
A
and
C
.
∎

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category whose morphisms are monomorphisms that has binary joins of subobjects. Suppose that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\displaystyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\textstyle\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\textstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt}{\scriptstyle\kern 2.80048pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 1.89871pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 2.80048pt}{\scriptscriptstyle\kern 1.42882pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\mid$\hss}\lower 0.96873pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle\smile$\hss}\kern 1.42882pt}}

satisfying invariance, monotonicity and base monotonicity. Then given any independent square like below on the left the square below on the right is independent.

.

and the independence of the required square follows by invariance and the fact that it does not matter whether we compute

.
